===== e68617d11383b6b70adb6ed2a2f82f26661241ce
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-15T18:15:49+00:00

P-1: memoise shift_day_demand_fit on RC5 (measurement only)

Step 1 of the agreed sequence. Applied to the RC5 engine
0e6f6435 -> b005d6b6, in both the release-candidate and fix-validation
packages.

shift_day_demand_fit is pure in (day, shift.index, start, duration): it
reads parsed.active, interval_minutes and intervals_per_day plus the
shift's own start and duration, none of which is written after
parse_input returns. Verified by grepping every assignment to those
fields. At most 7 x len(shifts) distinct answers exist - 168 on every
packaged workbook - and it was being recomputed 632,649 times per run,
with cyclic_requirement_context called 22.8 million times.

The cache lives on the parsed contract, so it is scoped to one run and
collected with it: no module-level state and no id() reuse hazard. The
original body is preserved verbatim as _shift_day_demand_fit_uncached.

Two correctness hazards were handled explicitly rather than assumed away.
demand_fit_blocked writes blank_requirement_blocked into the returned
dict, so every cache hit returns a fresh copy - sharing the object would
leak that flag between unrelated shifts. And the key carries start and
duration alongside the index, so a synthetic Shift reusing an index
cannot read another shift's answer, even though indices are unique and
contiguous in all eight packaged workbooks.

Proven inert, not assumed: capacity_diagnostics and
validate_input_contract output compared as canonicalised JSON across all
eight workbooks - IDENTICAL on every one, while total time fell from
36.07s to 6.67s (5.4x; per-workbook 2.6x to 8.5x).

Gate: 12 suites, 459 tests, PASS (was 11/453). Six new guards pin the
inertness claim and both hazards above.

The same function is also called inside
solve_joint_shift_off_language_break_refinement's search loop. That gain
is unmeasured and is not claimed.

FINAL_VALIDATION_AND_READINESS_REPORT.md deliberately still records the
base RC5 hash - it is the evidence for the build as originally validated
and was not rewritten to describe a build it did not test.
RELEASE_STATUS.json is unchanged: this patch does not alter the release
decision.

===== a8993d68d70777fbf635f914934aaa3d08d9256c
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-15T18:26:46+00:00

Adversarial engineering review of RC5

Challenges RC5 from the optimisation side rather than the release side.
Three measurements carry the argument.

The Stage-1 models are small: 5,880 binary variables at the largest,
49 at the smallest. CP-SAT solves personnel models two orders of
magnitude bigger to proven optimality. So four hours and
TRUNCATED_INSUFFICIENT_STAGE1_BUDGET is not a capacity limit.

The objective is a single weighted sum of 77 terms whose weights span
800,000,000 to 1 - target_miss at 3.2e9 against preference at 4. Terms
below about 1e6 are arithmetically inert, which is the A40 defect
generalised: A40 fixed one weight, the pattern remains across roughly
twenty more. It is also a fake lexicographic order that nobody can
verify by hand, and weighted-sum scalarization provably cannot reach
unsupported Pareto points, so better schedules exist that no weight
vector can return.

Symmetry is unbroken and astronomical. Grouping associates by the
attributes the model actually distinguishes, Cricut Chat has 24 of 27
associates mutually interchangeable - 6.2e23 relabelled twins of every
solution - with symmetry_level unset and no ordering constraints.

These connect: the weight spread ruins the relaxation, the symmetry
multiplies the search, so a tiny model cannot converge, so 17 weight
profiles were built to sample the frontier, so the budget truncates at
2 of 15, so results depend on machine load. One root cause chain.

Recommends lexicographic/epsilon-constraint objective, symmetry breaking
within interchangeable classes, deterministic budgets and a decision
strategy. Recommends against metaheuristics and column generation as
wrong for this scale, and against redesigning the architecture - the
foundation is right, the objective is what needs rebuilding.

States plainly what it could not do: no run artifacts have ever been
supplied, so output-quality and coverage-gain sections are answered
structurally and no improvement percentage is asserted as fact.

===== c0f7d3ca901c0bd91c0db208dd92bdfa422e3c0d
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-15T22:23:55+00:00

C-1: input-contract fidelity on RC5 (A-1, A-2, A-3, A-7)

Applied on top of P-1. Engine b005d6b6 -> 0b178297.

The rule enforced: absent may take a default, supplied-and-invalid must
fail closed. A value that was typed and then silently ignored is the
dangerous case, because it produces a plausible result no gate can catch.

A-1 _parse_break_segments. A contract stating zero breaks received a
hard-coded 15/30/15 - 60 minutes per associate - because an empty result
was backfilled. Out-of-set durations were coerced to 15 and negative
counts swallowed by max(0, ...). Break minutes drive required headcount
through need * shift_q / (shift_q - break_q), so this sized the roster
for a business agreement nobody signed. Zero now means zero; invalid now
raises HARD_INVALID_BREAK_CONTRACT, which the existing generic HARD_ rule
already turns into a preflight failure.

A-2 _detect_interval_minutes. A value outside {15,30,60} was discarded in
favour of inference, and inference returns a plausible 60. Interval
granularity is the denominator of every coverage number in the release,
so it is not recoverable downstream. Absent still infers; supplied-and-
invalid now raises HARD_INVALID_INTERVAL_MINUTES.

A-3 _parse_language_days. norm() lowercases and trims but keeps inner
spaces, so the aliases alldays/everyday/7days could never match "All
Days", "Every Day" or "7 Days" - the spellings people actually write -
and those were rejected as malformed. Third recurrence of the A51
assumption, so it is fixed at the root with a new _alias_key() helper
rather than worked around in the workbook again.

A-7 language_rules_at. A day outside 0..6 matched nothing, making "no
language rules apply anywhere" the silent default for a caller that
computed the day wrongly - which is how day_scope == 7 once emptied the
repair planner's donor set. The cyclic next-Sunday scope now maps to
Sunday and anything genuinely out of range raises.

Proven inert on every contract that exists today. All 8 packaged and both
live workbooks: preflight status unchanged, break segments unchanged,
interval unchanged, and the 120-field canonical contract hash IDENTICAL.

Gate: 13 suites, 475 tests, PASS (was 12/459). Sixteen new guards pin each
defect from both sides - the invalid value is rejected, the valid value
still works, and an absent value still takes its historical default.

Deliberately NOT done: symmetry breaking, deterministic budgets and
decision strategy. Those change solver behaviour, and changing the thing
being measured before a baseline exists is the mistake this review has
been warning about. They belong after the frozen-workbook A/B.

RELEASE_STATUS.json untouched: still NO_GO, production_ready false.

Review of the RC5 vs RC9.1 AE comparison

Recomputed supply and demand for all six AE workbooks from the shipped
RC5 engine. Two of the five compared cases are capacity-infeasible, which
changes how the aggregate table should be read.

  AE_IT_Choice  8 HC, 11 leave days -> 232 productive h vs 279 needed, -47h
  AE_IT_B2B     13 HC, 3 leave days -> 496 h vs 524 needed, -28h
  AE_FR_Choice  14 HC, 2 leave days -> 544 h vs 441 needed, +103h

Their AE_IT_Choice diagnosis is wrong on all three points. All eight
associates are English, so there is no language bottleneck; Friday
08:00-14:00 needs 2 to 3 of 8, so that window is not binding; and the real
cause is 11 of 40 associate-days on leave, 27.5% of the roster-week. The
engine's own preflight already says this at -46.6111 slack hours. Their
proposed remedy also fails: a no-break exception recovers one hour per
associate-day, so closing 46.6 hours needs about 47 exceptions, not one.
Their broader point that some failures are genuine business infeasibility
is correct - the mechanism and the fix are not.

AE_IT_B2B is where they attribute RC5's largest after-break loss, but at
-28 hours neither engine can reach target there, so that difference is
not evidence about break placement.

Confirms AE_FR_Choice as the right next test, for a reason they did not
state: it is the only case with ample capacity, equal before-break
coverage and a measurable after-break gap, so it is the only one where a
difference actually measures the optimiser.

Corrects their reading of the language-window result: all six workbooks
parse to mode OFF, so the tests show nothing about its impact either way.

Verified: the 672-interval claim is exact, all six input hashes and the
engine hash match PACKAGE_IDENTITY.json, and AE_IT_Choice runs a 0.85
target while the other five run 1.0.

Still not verifiable: no result artifacts and no RC9.1 package were
uploaded, so their section 5 table cannot be recomputed and the question
of whether the modernized input architecture changed the problem
definition stays open.

===== dc0e66754f512b41dc5670d5fa9d32ff23682386

AE results: the coverage root cause, located

First handoff with run artifacts. Recomputed all six from source: every RC5
number in their table verifies exactly, on engine 0e6f6435, ~3470s per run.
Their RC5 side is accurate. The conclusion drawn from it is not.

Grouped by the capacity class computed earlier, 15 of the 21 lost
after-break intervals - 71% - occur on AE_IT_B2B, which is short by 28.4
productive hours. On a roster that cannot cover its demand, taking an
hour of breaks per person must cost coverage; that is arithmetic. On the
three capacity-ample cases the total loss is 6 intervals, all on one case,
and two of three lose nothing.

AE_FR_Choice is the one genuine optimiser defect, and the engine proves
it against itself: break_capacity_status SUFFICIENT, deficit 0,
additional_headcount_for_breaks 0, headcount_needed equal to headcount
held - and target_losses_from_breaks 6. Every break could have been placed
without giving up an interval. Six were given up anyway.

It is not candidate generation, retention or selection - the three
suspects listed first in their section 8. global_best_before_target is
112 and final_before_target_loss_vs_global_best is 0, so the selector
kept the globally best skeleton and the loss is entirely inside break
placement.

zero_target_slack_ratio predicts which cases fail: 1.8% and 3.6% lose
nothing, 40.2% loses six with capacity available, 85.7% and 93.5% are
capacity-short and forced. AE_FR_Choice sits in the hard middle.

Every one of the five completed runs truncated at 3 of 15 skeleton
profiles, so the break stage chose from 3 skeletons rather than 15.

Two before-breaks-only runs of AE_FR_Choice reproduce 112 exactly and
each other, so the skeleton stage is not where the run-to-run variance
lives.

Also records a new defect: both before-breaks-only runs returned rc=4
with FAIL_METRIC_PARITY on a SKELETON_ONLY_COMPLETE artifact, suggesting
the parity gate compares after-break metrics a skeleton-only run never
produces.

Ships the capacity-benchmark gate with 10 guards. Gate: 14 suites, 485
tests.

AE_FR_Choice A/B complete: two corrections, one to my own earlier claim

Four runs of AE_FR_Choice on RC5+P1+C1, 900s each, sequential on an idle
machine.

Correction to myself: RC5 is deterministic. Two identical FULL runs agree
on 137 of 139 metrics, the two exceptions being an internal counter with
no effect on the outcome and a path string. Every coverage, floor, gap,
break and overage figure is identical, and the before-breaks pair both
returned 112. Earlier in this project I reported the same workbook at the
same seed producing a shippable schedule on one run and none on the next,
and carried that forward as a standing reliability risk. It does not
reproduce. Those two runs had been executing concurrently on one
container. The mechanism is machine load consuming a wall-clock budget,
not seed dependence, so the rule is narrower than I stated and their
existing results are more trustworthy than I claimed.

Unexpected finding: the budget split between stages costs more than break
placement. At the same 900s budget, BEFORE_BREAKS_ONLY reaches a 112
skeleton while FULL_SCHEDULE completes zero of fifteen profiles and
settles for 105 - seven intervals worse before breaks are placed at all.
Break placement costs four to six. The larger single loss is upstream of
the stage that has been under investigation.

Also notes that break loss rises with skeleton quality - the 105 skeleton
lost four intervals to breaks, the 112 skeleton lost six - because a
tighter skeleton leaves less slack for breaks to land in. Net still
favours the better skeleton by five.

===== 8ac72816f84c9e3a852ef7468e9de0c965c81564
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-16T19:54:35+00:00

B-1/B-2: make the release gates know which stage produced the artifact

Every BEFORE_BREAKS_ONLY run failed its own gates on a schedule with
nothing wrong with it. Four defects, all the same mistake — a gate
written for one stage applied to the other:

  B-1  The parity gate read selected_candidate.metrics, which only a
       FULL_SCHEDULE run creates. A skeleton run published nothing, and
       the gate read "did not publish" as "disagrees": 41 mismatches,
       every one engine: null, rc=4 FAIL_METRIC_PARITY.
  B-1c The quality report published active_intervals: 0 and safety:
       NOT_RUN for a run that covered 112 of 112. A null is a visible
       absence; 0 is a number, and it was wrong.
  B-1d The preliminary quality report runs before validation, was
       honestly blocked at that moment, and latched a code the later
       clean report could not clear.
  B-2  The run reported "No final schedule workbook was produced, so
       independent validation could not run" — on a run whose validation
       had run and passed.

The engine now publishes the surface for the stage it ran. Nothing new
is computed: the skeleton already carried one from calculate_metrics
with an empty break set, the same evaluator that scores every
after-break candidate, so parity is not the engine checking itself.

No gate became easier to pass. All 41 fields are compared at both
stages and three checks were added: the two sides must agree on the
stage, an unpublished surface is not evidence
(ENGINE_PUBLISHED_NO_METRIC_SURFACE), and on an artifact with no breaks
every after_X must equal its before_X twin — a side that invents a break
effect the artifact cannot contain is now caught rather than left for a
reader to interpret. The validator also reads a second, independent
stage signal and records a hard failure when the two contradict.

The stage gate is not the release gate. production_release_eligible is
always false for a skeleton, stated explicitly, and quality observations
(this run: REVIEW_EXTREME_OVERAGE) are reported rather than used to
block a diagnostic.

A first version of the fallback would have let AE_IT_Choice — a full run
that failed break placement — publish its before-break coverage as an
after-break result. The replay caught it; the fallback now applies only
to a run that genuinely stopped at Stage 1, pinned by its own test.

Proof:
  skeleton, same workbook/seed: rc 4 -> 0, parity FAIL(41) -> PASS(0),
    Phase C coverage 0/0 -> 112/112, safety NOT_RUN -> PASS
  full, same workbook/seed/budget vs its recorded baseline: rc 0,
    105/101 target, 112/110 floor, 2 floor gaps, same Phase C verdict —
    every figure reproduces
  all 8 recorded FULL_SCHEDULE audits replayed old vs new: identical
    return codes and identical reported values
  gate: 15 suites, 546 tests, PASS

No scheduling decision changed. No constraint, objective or search
budget was touched. B-3 through B-7 remain open; what this unlocks is
the fast diagnostic mode that exposed B-3 in the first place.

===== 15d63048c798e2b75bf59873d8d93bfedbd5a333
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-16T20:19:00+00:00

B-7: give the workbook a route to the parameters that shape the search

43 behavioural engine parameters -- search shape, reserve budgets, quality
minimums, solver seed -- existed only as command-line flags. The engine is
meant to be workbook-driven, so a scheduler working from the business
contract could not reach any of them.

Adding workbook rows alone would have fixed nothing. The production runner
passes a value for 24 of those parameters on every run, taken from its own
defaults and its depth arithmetic, and on a command line a default is
indistinguishable from a typed argument -- so the runner's opinion would
have silently outranked the contract every time. B-3 is an instance: the
Stage-1 portfolio is 15 profiles because the runner hardcodes 15, and at
900s the budget funds 0 of them, with no way to say so from the workbook.

Precedence is the rule the protected-tier minimums already follow:
explicit command line > workbook > engine default. A parameter neither
side sets keeps exactly the value it had, which rests on being able to
tell "absent" from "set to the default" -- argparse skips a default for an
attribute already on the namespace it is handed, so pre-seeding every
destination with None leaves unset options as None. The probe uses
parse_known_args, never parse_args: it must not be the thing that
terminates the process.

The runner now drops a flag when the workbook states that parameter,
unless the operator typed it on the runner's own command line. For the 18
flags it computes from its depth and exposes no option for, the workbook
always wins. search_control_decisions in the audit records what was set
and which side won.

An unreadable control row is a contract failure, not a fallback: a
scheduler who typed a value into the contract must not have it silently
discarded. strict_bool was added rather than reusing yes(), which reads
anything unrecognized as False -- right for a legacy row, wrong where a
typo would disable a search phase.

Proof:
  engine command byte-identical when the workbook is silent -- the old
    runner's flag list executed against the same args: 24 pairs, no
    value differences
  canonical contract hash unchanged on all 14 workbooks available
    (8 packaged + 6 AE); search_controls empty on every one
  route works end to end: 3 rows in Engine Defaults, runner reports them
    and stands down, audit shows seed 4242 and 2 skeleton profiles from
    the workbook against the runner's 9000 and 15; rc 0, validation PASS,
    parity PASS
  a bad value fails closed: HARD_INVALID_SEARCH_CONTROL, contract FAIL
  gate: 16 suites, 580 tests, PASS

No scheduling decision changed. Every default is where it was; this adds
a way to state a different one. Does not fix B-3 -- it removes the reason
B-3 could not be worked around from the workbook, and gives the B-3
measurement a way to vary the portfolio without editing the runner.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Claude-Session: https://claude.ai/code/session_01MH973LepRRvS7jQvRfGiqr

===== 8b4a781711fd7f088488ac3fbad88c23a5916140
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-16T22:05:39+00:00

B-3: the Stage-1 portfolio refuses to start a profile it could finish

Corrects my own earlier diagnosis. I reported B-3 as "Stage 1 starved by
the budget split" with B-5 (the 800M:1 weighted sum) as root cause. The
measurement does not support that and I should not have asserted the
chain before measuring it.

On AE_FR_Choice at 900s the Stage-1 allocation is 405s, the portfolio
starts with 171s left, and Stage 1 uses 67 solver-seconds while Stage 2
uses 760. The abandoned window is not discarded -- phase deadlines are
absolute, so time Stage 1 does not spend flows to Stage 2, which consumed
all of it. Stage 2 was not short of time; it was polishing a worse
skeleton. One line stops the portfolio:

    if remaining < STAGE1_MIN_MEANINGFUL_SLICE_SEC:   # 171 < 240
        break                                         # 0 of 15 attempted

The 240s constant is well-evidenced -- on AE AR B2B a profile returns
UNKNOWN at 150s and 166/168 at 210s. The defect is that a floor
calibrated on the hardest workbook gates whether to attempt anything at
all. AE_FR_Choice reaches OPTIMAL in 13.4s; the run had 171 seconds and
spent zero. The helper's docstring anticipates the easy case, but that
reasoning only applies once a profile is allowed to start.

Measured, same workbook/seed/budget, floor stated from the workbook via
the B-7 route so both runs use identical code:

                        floor 240      floor 30
  profiles attempted    0 of 15        5 of 15
  before_target         105            112   (+7)
  after_target          101            109   (+8)
  after_floor           110            112
  floor gaps            2              0
  validation / parity   PASS / PASS    PASS / PASS

112 is exactly what BEFORE_BREAKS_ONLY reaches, so the +7 is skeleton
quality that was always available and never generated. It also shrinks
B-4: target_losses_from_breaks falls from 6 to 3, so part of what looked
like break-placement loss was break placement doing its best from a
skeleton worth 105.

Changed: the floor is now reachable as the workbook row "Stage 1 Minimum
Slice Seconds" and --stage1-minimum-slice-sec, under the B-7 precedence.
stage1_slice_seconds and stage1_fundable_profile_count take it as a
parameter and keep every guarantee at any value.

NOT changed: the default is still 240. One easy workbook is not grounds
to move a constant calibrated on the hardest one. The outstanding
measurement is AE_AR_B2B floor 240 vs 30 at the same budget; its baseline
shows the identical shape (funds=0, 0 of 15 attempted), so the floor is
not currently protecting it either. That measurement decides the default.

The likely fix is not a smaller constant: the floor is right about depth
and wrong about using depth as a precondition for attempting. When the
portfolio would otherwise attempt zero profiles, spend the remaining
window on one -- zero is categorically different from fewer, and the cost
is bounded to a single slice. That will be measured on both workbooks
before it becomes a default.

Contract hash unchanged on all 14 workbooks. Gate: 16 suites, 582 tests,
PASS.

===== d49fc4fab5f4f944b37e768e4d8002e7e17285e2
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-16T22:48:32+00:00

B-8: the validator was checking a weaker next-Sunday rule under the same name

Found by the parity gate on AE_AR_B2B at 900s: engine 1, validator 0, and
all 40 other canonical fields matching exactly, reproducibly on both runs
of an unrelated A/B pair. The schedule was fine; the checker was not.

The solver penalises next-Sunday adjacency between neighbouring QUARTER
slots and the engine metric measures exactly that. The independent
validator compared adjacent INTERVALS using each interval's maximum. On a
60-minute workbook that is a quarter of the adjacent pairs, and an
interval maximum cannot see a staffing cliff inside the hour. The
validator was not independently confirming the rule the engine enforces;
it was confirming a weaker rule sharing its name -- the failure the
canonical metric surface exists to prevent, one level below naming: the
names matched, the measurements did not.

The validator already computed the per-quarter counts and discarded the
sequence, keeping only the maximum. It now records the sequence against
its quarter-slot index and walks adjacent quarters with the solver's own
guard. It still computes every raw count itself from workbook cells --
sharing which quarters are protected is contract, sharing the measurement
would make the gate compare the engine to itself.

This makes the checker stricter: it can now see violations it previously
could not.

Verified on six artifacts across two workbooks and both stages. The two
AE_AR_B2B artifacts now read 1 against the engine's 1 and pass with zero
hard failures; the four AE_FR_Choice artifacts read 0 against 0, so no
false positives were introduced.

Note for the earlier AE comparison package: those six runs were validated
with the under-reporting checker, so a clean
next_sunday_imbalance_violation_count there is weaker evidence than it
looks. Coverage numbers are unaffected -- this is a balance metric.

Gate: 16 suites, 588 tests, PASS.

B-3: the Stage-1 slice floor was measured cold and applied to warm solves

Root cause, exactly. STAGE1_MIN_MEANINGFUL_SLICE_SEC = 240 was set from
tools/stage1_slice_depth_probe.py, which calls build_skeleton with no
hint_skeleton -- a cold start. The Stage-1 portfolio loop never solves
cold: it always passes hint_skeleton=select_stage1_hint_skeleton(...), so
by the time the portfolio runs, every profile is warm-started from the
feasibility probe and the deterministic baseline. The floor was
calibrated on a scenario the loop never creates.

The probe now has a warm mode that rebuilds the anchor the run holds.
Same workbook, profile and seed:

           cold                     warm
   15s     -                        UNKNOWN
   30s     -                        FEASIBLE 168/168
   45s     UNKNOWN                  FEASIBLE 168/168
  150s     UNKNOWN                  -
  210s     166/168                  -
  240s     -                        FEASIBLE 168/168

The cliff moves from 150-210s to 15-30s and warm quality saturates
immediately at 168/168 -- RC9.1's recorded best, and better than anything
the cold curve reached. The engine was refusing a 171-second window for a
solve that needed 30.

Default set to 45.0 by the same method that chose 240: clear the measured
cliff with margin. 45 clears the warm cliff by 50% and is itself measured
feasible at full quality. It is also reachable per roster via the B-7
route (workbook row "Stage 1 Minimum Slice Seconds").

End to end, shipped default vs shipped default, 900s seed 9000:

  AE_FR_Choice      attempted 0/15 -> 3/15
                    before 105 -> 112   after 101 -> 109
                    floor 112/110 -> 112/112   gaps 2 -> 0
  AE_AR_B2B         attempted 0/15 -> 3/15
  (240's own        before 166 -> 168   after 161 -> 164
   calibration      floor 168/167 -> 168/168   gaps 1 -> 0
   workbook)        rc 4 -> 0

The feared risk did not materialise: every 45s warm slice on the hard
workbook returned FEASIBLE, and that workbook now reaches its maximum
before-break coverage and closes its last floor gap. 112 and 168 are
exactly what BEFORE_BREAKS_ONLY reaches, so these are skeleton quality
that was always available and never generated.

Recorded honestly: an exploratory floor-30 run reached after_target 167
on AE_AR_B2B against the shipped 45's 164, both above the baseline 161.
One point either way is inside run-to-run variation of a wall-clock
search; it is a reason to re-tune per roster with the workbook control,
not grounds to claim 30 beats 45.

Two selector tests encoded the cold measurement as an invariant ("45s is
the slice at which AE AR B2B produced no skeleton at all"). That premise
is false warm, as measured here. Their intent is preserved and the
numbers corrected: the floor must clear the point where a solve produces
nothing (now 15s, warm), and a small window must not claim to fund the
whole portfolio while still reporting what it honestly can.

Also shrinks B-4: target_losses_from_breaks on AE_FR_Choice falls 6 -> 3.

Author: Claude

B-4, B-5, B-6: measured; one real defect, two mischaracterised by me

B-4 -- REAL DEFECT, headroom proved exactly.
After B-3, AE_FR_Choice loses 3 target intervals to breaks, not 6. A probe
reconstructs the run's exact skeleton from its own before-break workbook
(112/112/112, faithful) and asks solve_breaks for a lossless placement:

  min_target_hits 112  ->  INFEASIBLE in 1.6s   (proof, not a timeout)
  min_target_hits 111  ->  FEASIBLE, hard-clean, 0 exceptions

The provable minimum is 1 loss; the engine shipped 3. Two intervals were
left on the table. Not capacity (BREAK_CAPACITY_SUFFICIENT is correct), not
candidate generation or selection (final_before_target_loss_vs_global_best
= 0). The phase whose job this is never ran:
run_target_lock_recovery_phase needs 65s at its entry guard, was allocated
19s, and attempted 0 of 14 eligible candidates. Its ceiling at a 900s
budget is 53s even when Stage 1 asks for nothing, so at QUICK no setting
can fund it.

I wrote a fix for that and reverted it. It dropped any phase allocated
below its entry guard and returned the seconds to break search. My own
data disproved the premise: phase deadlines are CUMULATIVE, so a phase
inherits what earlier phases underran -- post_break_repair held a nominal
19s against a 35s guard on that same run and still attempted a repair.
Dropping on nominal allocation would have deleted real work. What is kept
is the diagnostic only, with no allocation change: audit
budget_phase_viability plus a BUDGET_PHASE_AT_RISK log line, stating
explicitly that a nominal shortfall is not proof of starvation and must be
read beside the phase's own attempted count. Funding target_lock_recovery
at QUICK is a real trade-off against Stage-1 and break search and is left
open, not guessed.

B-5 -- measured precisely, no symptom, no change.
Read off the built model, not the declared weights: 2,109 objective terms,
14 distinct coefficients, 8 to 3,200,000,000, range 400,000,000:1. Tier by
tier against each variable's domain, 13 of the 14 tiers can be outvoted in
aggregate by the tier below, so the ladder does not implement the priority
order it resembles. It does not need to: candidate order is a real
lexicographic tuple and guarantees are hard constraints (min_target_hits
is what the B-4 proof above uses). Worst-case objective across the eight
packaged rosters is 7.0e12-1.5e13, about 600,000x int64 headroom. My
earlier report named B-5 as the root cause of B-3; the B-3 measurement
disproved that. Restructuring a solver objective on a measurement with no
symptom is the speculative change this work has avoided throughout.

B-6 -- NOT a defect; my description was wrong twice.
The symmetry is real: Cricut_Chat has 24 of 27 associates in one orbit,
6.204e23 equivalent permutations, confirming the figure. But
symmetry_level is not "unset" in the sense I implied -- OR-Tools defaults
it to 2, the enabled level. And the symmetry is not unbroken: presolve
finds it and installs an orbitope.

  Cricut_Chat  1047 generators, 613 orbits sized 24, orbitope 137 x 24
  NMG_EN_PROD   409 generators, 316 orbits sized 10, orbitope  94 x 10

That is the canonical strongest break for interchangeable columns and
exactly what hand-written symmetry breaking would try to add. Adding it
manually would duplicate or conflict with it. No change made.

Shipped behaviour changed: none. Diagnostic added: one. Fix written and
reverted: one. The undefined-name sweep caught a real bug I introduced
(audit referenced before assignment) before any run did.

Gate: 17 suites, 607 tests, PASS.

===== be690491d72246f0ea5fa9b3d78295b3a698c179

Scope the fork instead of carrying "never merged" as a status

The two engines have been forked since Coverage Split was built and nobody
has said what reconciling them involves. Read off the code:

  this repo   L6.3.2.6-...-RC1  21,204 lines  57 coverage_split references
  RC5         L6.3.2.7-...-RC2  21,409 lines   0

Direction is port Coverage Split INTO RC5, not the reverse: RC5 is the
release line carrying the hardened parsers, canonical metric surface,
parity gate, validator and packaging, plus everything fixed this session.
Coverage Split is one feature; the hardening is a lineage.

The port is 7 self-contained definitions plus ~50 call sites across 8
existing functions -- and all 8 exist in the RC5 lineage, so there is no
structural conflict, only integration. 38 test references and one fixture
come with it.

One part is not mechanical: calculate_metrics is both the densest site
(11) and the function whose output becomes the canonical metric surface
the parity gate checks. Adding coverage_split_* fields there means either
teaching the independent validator to recompute them from workbook cells
at the granularity the solver constrains, or excluding them from
PARITY_FIELDS deliberately. Silently adding engine-only metrics is exactly
the shape of B-8.

Sequenced after the Stage-1 default sweep: if that reverts the floor, the
base being ported onto changes.

Author: Claude

B-9: analyse all sixteen unchecked decision-driving metrics ahead of the sweep

Checked each one the way B-8 was found: compare what the metric measures
against what the solver constrains, looking for a basis, granularity or
scope mismatch.

Two real issues, neither a safety hole:

  week_boundary_hard_failure_count sums five overlapping categories, so a
  quarter that is zero-staffed AND a language gap AND an opening gap counts
  three times. It is not the count it appears to be. Every consumer tests
  == 0, so over-counting can only cause a false failure, never a false
  pass.

  hard_floor_gap_count compares raw ratios while the solver enforces
  ceiled units, making the metric systematically weaker than the
  constraint it reports on. Not reachable today -- the only two paths that
  relax hard_floor are diagnostic and never produce release candidates --
  but it becomes reachable the moment one does.

Checked specifically for a B-8 repeat in the current-week balance metric:
whole_week_adjacent_rows iterates quarter slots, matching the solver. Not
a repeat.

Build order favours what is both consequential and cheap. First six close
both hard-validity metrics, the release-gating loss cap and B-8's unchecked
sibling:

  1 target_losses_from_breaks          trivial  gates release, ranks
                                                candidates, is the B-4
                                                quantity, inputs present
  2 before_severe_floor_gap_count      trivial  after-side already checked
  3 week_boundary_hard_failure_count   near-free all five parts present
  4 week_boundary_max_adjacent_raw_change near-free B-8 sequence exists
  5 week_boundary_max_coverage_ratio   easy
  6 hard_floor_gap_count               easy

transferable_overstaffing_pair_count is flagged as the highest risk of a
fake check: an independent version means reimplementing the pairing
heuristic, and copying the engine's would produce a parity field that can
never disagree -- worse than uncovered, because it looks covered.

No metric was found returning a wrong value; this is static analysis of
basis against constraint. Implementation waits for the AE sweep, which
invokes the validator per case.

===== 128c1a5f9d430a8a408fa33fa8b9c951293a4544
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-17T01:31:36+00:00

B-10: the adaptive break search never runs; a cap is being used as a floor

The 112-task adaptive break search completes 0 attempts on essentially
every run, including the RC5 authors' own recorded 3600s runs:

  B3_FRC_DEFAULT45  112 planned,  0 completed, proposed 151.0s
  B4_DIAG           112 planned,  0 completed, proposed 114.7s
  AE_AR_Choice*     140 planned,  0 completed, proposed  68.1s
  AE_IT_B2B*        112 planned,  2 completed, proposed 112.0s
  AE_FR_Choice*     112 planned,  3 completed, proposed 105.4s   (*authors, 3600s)

Longer budgets truncate harder, because a longer run plans a bigger
portfolio and divides the window into smaller slices.

Root cause: BREAK_MIN_MEANINGFUL_SLICE_SEC = 180 gates whether to attempt
at all, but its own recorded justification is a ceiling argument --
verbatim, "180 is the point past which more time per attempt buys
nothing". The same curve puts the feasibility cliff at 90s and shows 150s
returning a feasible 160. Stage 1 has both a floor and a cap
(STAGE1_MAX_SLICE_SEC); the break search has one constant, justified as a
cap, doing a floor's job.

On B3_FRC_DEFAULT45 the search stopped at 549s inside a 471-700s window,
leaving 151 seconds of its own phase -- precisely the slice it refused.
Cumulative deadlines pass that to joint refinement, so it is not destroyed,
but a 112-task portfolio is silently traded for one narrower operator.

Secondarily this is B-3's defect again: break_slice_depth_probe.py calls
solve_breaks with no hint_solution (cold) while the adaptive loop passes
hint_solution (warm). Unlike B-3, no new measurement is needed to act --
the recorded cold curve already shows feasibility at 90s.

Interaction worth stating: B-3 raised the surviving skeleton count, which
grew the break portfolio from 28 planned to 112 and took completions from
1 to 0. B-3 still produced the better schedule (112/109 vs 105/101), so
this is not an outcome regression, but the value of fixing B-10 went up
when B-3 landed.

Proposed: split the constant as Stage 1 does -- floor 90-120 (cliff plus
margin), cap 180 -- then A/B with repeats, since B-4 showed run-to-run
variance is the same size as the effect.

Also recorded: the Stage-1 retention cap never binds (1-7 retained against
16 across eleven runs), and deterministic_target_baseline has a fixed 60s
floor that overrides a 45s budget-derived share at QUICK.

 evidence/B10_BREAK_SLICE_FLOOR_IS_A_CAP.md | 168 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 168 insertions(+)
===== 6e62c0a03feb3c60907a6682fb2429c7663c5d4f
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-17T01:35:04+00:00

B-11: a misspelled name silently deletes approved leave

Three input sheets match rows to the roster by exact normalized name and
drop unmatched rows. The engine has no fuzzy or near-match helper of any
kind, so any spelling variation between weeks is an exact miss.

The Preference sheet is the worst and is completely silent:

    assoc = by_name.get(norm(ws.cell(r, name_col).value))
    if assoc:
        assoc.preferences = [...]
    # no else, no warning

Preferences are not advisory -- they carry leave and hard OFF:

    if parsed.leave_enabled and preference_kind_value == "leave": return []
    if parsed.hard_off  and preference_kind_value == "off":   return []

So a name typed differently from the roster discards that person's approved
leave and guaranteed OFF days, they become available to the solver, and
they get scheduled. No warning, contract passes, quality gate passes,
independent validation passes, production_eligible.

Fixed Request is half guarded: it warns only when ZERO rows match, so one
bad row among many is silent. Previous week scheduled raises an advisory
and leaves previous_saturday empty, and previous_saturday_compatible("")
returns True -- the Sunday rest-gap carry-in silently disappears. On that
same sheet a DUPLICATE name is HARD_ while an UNKNOWN name is advisory,
which ranks duplicates above a dropped rest constraint.

Why nothing catches it: the independent validator parses the same input
contract, sees the same absent leave and OFF, and correctly concludes the
schedule satisfies every rule it can see. Both sides are consistently
wrong. Parity verifies that the engine computed the contract correctly,
never that the contract is what the scheduler wrote -- so this class is
invisible to that architecture by construction, unlike B-8 where the two
sides disagreed.

Fix: join the existing fail-closed convention (27 HARD_ codes already).
Report unmatched rows on every name-matched sheet; make them HARD_ for
Preference and Fixed Request; resolve the duplicate/unknown asymmetry on
the carry-in sheet. Near-match detection would give a better message but is
not required. Check the departed-staff workflow first, so failing closed
does not block legitimate runs.

No packaged workbook triggers this today -- all eight parse clean. The
defect is that nothing would tell you when one does.

 evidence/CONSOLIDATED_STATE_AND_REMEDIATION.md | 186 +++++++++++++++++++++++++

===== 6cf3b63f6776f54382d4e312e43d92b007923b0a
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-17T01:48:07+00:00

B-9 (staged): independent check for six decision-driving metrics

Six metrics that decide hard validity or candidate selection were computed
only by the engine. The parity gate could not catch an engine-side error in
any of them, because no second derivation of the number existed to disagree.

This stages the fix rather than applying it: the AE sweep is currently
importing the release tree and invokes the validator once per case, so
changing that code mid-sweep would validate early and late cases with
different code and make the whole comparison meaningless.

tools/apply_b9_metric_coverage.py
  Makes the independent validator derive all seven fields (the six plus
  floor_losses_from_breaks, free from the same loop) from the workbook rows
  it already parses, and adds them to the canonical surface: 41 -> 48
  compared fields. Aborts rather than guessing if an anchor is not unique.

  Two semantics are matched deliberately rather than approximately:
  - hard_floor_gap_count is 0 when no hard floor is configured, mirroring
    the engine. The validator's HARD_FLOOR *failure* falls back to the soft
    floor; the metric must not, or every workbook without a hard floor would
    report a false mismatch.
  - week_boundary_max_adjacent_raw_change is a max over ALL adjacent pairs.
    The validator previously recorded only the violating deltas, which would
    have reported 0 on a clean schedule where the engine reports the real
    largest step.
  - week_boundary_max_coverage_ratio is excluded from INTEGER_FIELDS: it is
    a ratio, and integer comparison would hide every drift below 1.0.

tests_staged/test_rc9_2_4_b9_metric_coverage.py
  Written from both sides: the gate must report a disagreement and must not
  report a false one. Two further tests compare the derivations against real
  recorded artifacts, where a disagreement is the point of B-9 and must be
  root-caused, not tolerated.

Verified on a copy of the tree: 8/8 synthetic tests pass patched, and fail
with 27 failures/errors unpatched, so they are not vacuous. The artifact
tests run once the patch is applied to the live tree after the sweep.

Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-17T02:02:36+00:00

B-11 + B-12 (staged): name-matched sheets fail closed, header anchored

B-11 makes the three name-matched input sheets report unmatched rows instead
of dropping them. Preference rows carry approved LEAVE and hard OFF, so a name
typed differently from the roster currently discards that person's leave, makes
them available to the solver, and schedules them, with no warning anywhere. The
parity architecture cannot catch this by construction: the independent
validator parses the same contract, sees the same absent leave, and correctly
concludes the schedule satisfies every rule it can see.

Validating B-11 against the 15-workbook corpus surfaced two things.

B-12, a second defect, which had to land first. _find_header_row was hardened
once before, after a prose banner satisfied ["language", "minimum"] on its own;
that fix requires the terms in DISTINCT cells, which does nothing for a single
term. All three name-matched sheets look up ["name"], and the NMG13 Preference
banner contains the word "name", so row 2 is chosen over the real header on
row 4. Today that is latent rather than live: every column falls back to
positional guessing that happens to match the real layout, so the right cells
are still read. It is the same luck the _day_columns docstring already calls
out. But with B-11 in place the header text "SF Name" is read as a data row and
two shipped workbooks fail their contract over a column title.

The fix prefers, among rows that already pass the term test, one whose headers
bind all seven weekdays -- a structural property, not a guess about prose. It
is strictly additive and enabled only at the three day-bearing call sites.
Measured: 0 of 15 workbooks change contract hash or preference values.

An empty/populated split in B-11, driven by the corpus rather than assumed.
SAKS_NEW ships a 51st Preference row against a 50-person roster with every day
cell empty. An unmatched row that carries no value asserts no constraint, so
failing the contract over it is noise. An unmatched row that DOES carry a value
is the dangerous case and stays a contract failure: the engine cannot tell a
misspelling from a departure, and that is the reason to make a human reconcile
rather than to guess.

The one escape is a NAMED list, "Known Departed Associates" in Instructions.
Not a suppression flag: each name is written out, and acknowledged rows are
still reported as visible warnings.

Corpus result: 14 of 15 workbooks clean. SAKS_NEW reports its empty Preference
row as a soft warning and its populated previous-Saturday row ('22:00 - 07:00'
for a name not on the roster) as a hard failure. That last one is a genuine
finding in a shipped regression asset and is NOT being edited away -- whether
'Associate 051' is a typo or a departure is the authors' call.

Both are staged, not applied: the AE sweep is importing the release tree.

Gate on the combined tree: 17 suites PASS, 2 selfchecks PASS, undefined-name
sweep PASS.

Section evaluation S1/S4: the contract layer fails open in two ways

Section-by-section evaluation of the engine, starting from a structural map
built from the AST: 403 top-level objects, 14 engine sections, 3 satellite
modules. Cross-cutting scans first, to allocate reading effort by evidence.

C-2 (HIGH). yes() is the only parser for instruction booleans and has no
unrecognised state: any non-empty text outside eight accepted words is silently
False, with no warning, so validate_input_contract has nothing to see.

Measured on a copy of GDI_REAL28 (the shipped asset was not modified): of eight
affirmative-intent values a planner might type into the Leave cell, four --
including 'yes.' and 'Enable' -- set leave_enabled to False. Zero warnings.
leave_enabled gates the hard rule that stops an associate working an approved
leave day, so every person on leave becomes schedulable.

Ten flags parse this way; seven default to an enabling value, so a typo removes
a constraint rather than failing to add one: leave, hard OFF, strict OFF,
separate OFF days, use preferences, fixed requests, opening guard. This is
strictly worse in reach than B-11 -- that loses one person's leave, this loses
everyone's from a single cell -- and parity cannot catch either, because the
independent validator parses the same contract.

The engine already solved this once: B-7's strict_bool emits
HARD_INVALID_SEARCH_CONTROL on text it does not recognise. It covers 2 call
sites; these 10 were never migrated. The fix needs no new machinery.

C-1 (LATENT, systemic). Gate limits are read through getattr(parsed, field,
default) in 90 places over 36 fields. Where the shadow default disagrees with
the declared one it is more permissive every time: cap ratio 2.0 against a
declared 1.35, and 999999 against declared limits of 0, 3 and 6. Eleven fields
where no shadow default matches; four where it differs between call sites.
Measured: raw cap 20 against 14, adjacent limit 999999 against 3.

Latent because ParsedInput is built in one place and never deserialized. It
goes live through test doubles -- eight test files build parsed as a
SimpleNamespace, so a test can pass on semantics production never uses -- or
through any future change making a field optional. One field is already
inconsistent today: quality_max_target_losses_from_breaks reads 6 at one call
site and 999999 at eight.

Also recorded, low severity: three dead determinism guards (the OR-Tools
attribute exists, assignment cannot raise, and the value set is already the
default); multi-worker nondeterminism, which the engine already documents
honestly at line 17975 and which bounds single-run A/B to effects larger than
one interval; 54 unreferenced RC-marker constants; an ambiguous branch in
minute_of_day; and to_float's 58 permissive call sites sitting beside
strict_float's 11, which exists precisely to avoid that.

No code changed. The AE sweep is importing the release tree.

===== 836add4d70d6c11b4943289afd5974943c38b433
Author: Claude

S5 evaluation: C-3, and a correction downgrading C-2

C-3 (HIGH). preference_kind recognises exactly two words -- "off" and "leave".
Everything else returns "other", and "other" falls through all three blocking
branches of associate_day_eligible_shifts, so the person is schedulable.

Measured: Annual Leave, Leave Day, A/L, AL, Vacation, Sick, Leave - approved,
Leve, OFF (approved), Off Day, Rest, RD and X all produce no constraint and no
warning. The Preference sheet carries zero data validations in every shipped
workbook, and its own banner invites free text: "Optional: enter Leave/OFF/
shift preference by day."

Latent today -- 0 unrecognised values across all 15 workbooks, because those
sheets are machine-generated -- and live the first time a human types into the
sheet the engine asks them to type into. Parity is blind to it for the same
reason as B-11: the validator calls the engine's own preference_kind on the
same cell and correctly reports no rule was broken.

The fix is two parts and the second matters more: widen the vocabulary, but
above that stop treating "other" as silence. An unrecognised non-blank
preference cell is a value the planner meant something by and should fail
closed like B-11's unmatched name. C-3 is B-11 one level down -- a matched
name whose value is not understood -- so fixing B-11 alone leaves the cheaper
half of the hole open.

CORRECTION: C-2 downgraded HIGH -> MEDIUM. I checked the workbooks rather than
only the parser and was wrong. build_input_template.py puts a "Yes,No"
validation with errorStyle="stop" on all ten flags, and the shipped workbooks
carry it. GDI_REAL28 does have a Legacy Instructions sheet with three of them
as free text, but that sheet is never read -- the instruction map is built from
Engine Defaults and Instructions only, with Instructions winning. A planner
typing into Excel cannot produce the failure.

What survives: Excel validation is not enforced on paste, not enforced when a
workbook is written programmatically (how every regression asset here is
produced), and not enforced identically by LibreOffice or Google Sheets. So
C-2 is a defence-in-depth gap -- the dropdown is the only thing between a
mistyped cell and a silently removed hard constraint, and the parser beneath
it has no opinion -- rather than a live hole. Worth closing on that basis, not
on an imminent failure.

S2 and S6 evaluation: nesting-group UNSAT, an unreachable check, a clean result

S2 (checkpoint/resume): SOUND. run_id is hashed from input, contract, engine,
seed, parameters and git state, so a changed workbook can never resume onto
stale skeletons and no restored pattern index can drift under a changed break
window. write_json and write_csv are atomic, so the container death that
happened during this work could not have left a truncated checkpoint. The
comment at 17790 records a real earlier bug -- an absolute wall-clock deadline
hashed into run_id, which defeated the identity gate and made --resume
permanently inoperative -- and fixes it correctly.

S6-0 (positive): the Stage-1 objective is genuinely weighted-sum, not
accidentally lexicographic. For each of the 17 profiles I ordered the terms by
weight and checked whether any weight exceeds the maximum contribution of
everything beneath it, using real bounds from AE_AR_B2B. 343 weighted terms,
343 able to change an optimum. This bounds B-5: the non-separating-tier problem
is Stage-2 only.

S6-1 (latent, high if reached): leave[a,d] is pinned to a constant from the
contract at 5518, and nesting groups then add leave[member,d] ==

have different approved leave adds 1 == 0.

Measured on GDI_REAL28: control FEASIBLE; same group with different leave
INFEASIBLE in 0.04s. The log says only status=INFEASIBLE, diagnostics carry no
cause -- 'conflicts' is CP-SAT's conflict counter, not an explanation -- and
validate_input_contract reports no nesting failure or warning. The run dies in
40ms and nothing says why. OFF is only partly pinned, so it is specifically
leave that is fatal.

S6-2 (dead code): the one nesting-group contract check,

exclusive -- an exact-days row has its group cleared at 1511. Measured: 0

actually kills the model.

coverage from any real workbook. Both defects above live in that unexercised

 evidence/WHOLE_ENGINE_CONSOLIDATION.md | 205 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++

===== ed042f53d99e644f510bdd945d1eb171c90a8517
Author: Claude

Fast logic-check suite: 41 tests in 5ms, 8 of 8 mutations caught

Small direct checks for the engine's equations and predicates, written so a
wrong equation fails rather than snapshotting current behaviour. Pure Python --
no CP-SAT, no workbook, no IO -- so it can sit in front of every commit.

Covers: time and numeric primitives (hhmm, minute_of_day, parse_time_window,
inclusive windows including the overnight wrap, shift_parts, circular distance,
ceil_units, scaled factors, to_float vs strict_float, yes, safe_name, norm);
domain predicates (preference_kind, rest_compatible at the exact boundary,
previous_saturday_compatible, shift start step alignment, day-header decoys);
coverage and threshold math (quality limits, concurrent-break monotonicity,
fractional overage, cap-vs-requirement invariants); budget math; selection math
(deficit bucketing, protected tiers, secondary tiers, dominance irreflexivity
and antisymmetry); the canonical metric surface; and break-pattern legality.

Non-vacuity proven by mutation: eight deliberate defects introduced one at a
time into a copy of the engine -- circular distance taking the long way, the
rest boundary turning strict, the overnight window losing its wrap, OFF
preferences no longer recognised, the protected-tier bound flipping and to or,
shift duration losing the midnight case, ceil_units rounding down, and
dominance accepting equality. All eight fail the suite.

honest split.

field of 41 whose alias tuple omits its own name, so re-canonicalizing an
already-canonical surface silently drops it to MISSING_CANONICAL_METRIC.
compare_metric_surfaces canonicalizes whatever it is handed, so this is a live
trap for any new caller. Latent today because both sides emit one of the two
spellings that ARE aliases. Pinned by a test that fails if the list changes.

Mine: I assumed ceil_units and scaled_effective_factor default to scale 1 and
100 -- they default to 100 and JOINT_COVERAGE_SCALE. I asserted
stage1_fundable_profile_count must always report at least one profile, but its
docstring says "Reporting only: the loop does not truncate its portfolio up
front", so 0 at a tiny window is the correct statement. And I asserted
guaranteed_stage2_reserve_seconds cannot consume the whole run -- it can and
does at 300s, because it is an unclamped upper bound whose only consumer
clamps it; the invariant that matters is on bounded_stage2_guard_reserve_seconds,
which the engine already selfchecks at the same 45s floor. Those tests now
assert the documented semantics instead.

CP-SAT model verification: 9 rules probed, 9 enforced, 0 new defects

Closes a limit I had stated explicitly: I had read the constraint construction
but never established that the model actually enforces the rule set.

Method is binding-constraint probing. For each rule: the base contract must
solve, the rule made impossible must come back INFEASIBLE, and the same contract
with the rule switched off via HardConfig must solve again. All three together
prove the constraint is present, that it binds, and that the switch controls
exactly it.

Stage 1, 8 of 8: leave blocks work, hard OFF blocks work, strict OFF count,
max shift variety, rest gap between days, language minimum per interval,
opening minimum FTE, fixed shift legality. Every one INFEASIBLE with the rule on
and FEASIBLE with it off. Stage 2: language minimum survives break placement,
enforced.

Two of my three apparent findings were my own probe errors, and both corrections
are worth more than the findings would have been.

I reported a malformed fixed shift as silently accepted. It is not.
preference_kind("99:99 - 99:99") is "other", so the legality branch guarded by
kind == "shift" is never reached -- but validate_input_contract catches it as
UNKNOWN_FIXED_SHIFT, and the contract gate runs before any model is built
(validate at 17443, FAIL_PRE_SOLVER_CONTRACT at 18018, first build_skeleton at
18059). My probe called build_skeleton directly and fed the model a contract
that could never reach it. Re-probed with a well-formed shift absent from the
contract: MODEL_INVALID, correctly.

I reported the break concurrency cap as unenforced on an UNKNOWN result, which
is a time-limit artifact and should not have been printed as a verdict.
maximum_concurrent_breaks floors both caps at 1 by design -- "preserving at
least one working associate" -- so the zero cap my probe assumed is unreachable
through contract settings. The model was harder, not infeasible. Recorded as a
gap needing a better probe rather than claimed as a pass.

Still unproven and named as such: break concurrency, week-boundary carry-out,
blank-interval staffing, nesting group equality (no workbook exercises it), and
the coverage floor/target terms, which are soft objective terms rather than hard
constraints and cannot be probed this way.

was satisfiable, so not a binding probe.

naming: mutating a parsed contract after parsing is unreliable, because
parse_input derives interdependent structures -- demand-fit eligibility, opening
intervals, cyclic windows, coverage maps -- that a mutation of active flags or a
forced shift leaves stale, so the model goes infeasible for a reason other than
the rule under test. Every valid probe mutated only what the model reads
directly: preference cells, scalar settings, HardConfig switches. That is the
boundary of the technique without purpose-built fixtures, which is fix-plan item
W3 arrived at from another direction.

fixtures, and the coverage floor/target terms unprobeable because they are soft
objective terms. Zero new engine defects across both rounds.

CP-SAT round 3: break concurrency closed structurally, still zero new defects

Two more constructions failed before a valid one worked. Pinning every break
segment to one legal quarter did produce exactly one legal pattern, but made the
model INFEASIBLE even at a cap of 99 in warn mode: with everyone breaking at the
same quarter, coverage falls to zero and after_raw_expr >= 1 fails. Confounded
like the rest.

What worked, in two complementary parts.

Behaviourally: with free windows and an effective cap of 1, warn mode produced a
schedule violating the cap 23 times with max 3 concurrent, and a generous cap
produced 1 violation with max 4. So warn permits and records, which is the
designed semantics, and the configured cap genuinely drives the measurement.
fail mode returned UNKNOWN at 20s -- neither proven feasible nor infeasible, and
recorded as such rather than as a pass.

Structurally: rather than spend solver time the running sweep needs, I counted
what the model contains at a 1s limit. Same skeleton, same cap: warn builds
7,304 variables, fail builds 6,917, with constraint count identical at 5,073.
That 387-variable gap is exactly the expected signature -- warn creates one
concurrency_excess IntVar per quarter-slot plus the constraint defining it,
while fail adds break_count <= cap for the same slots with no slack variable.
The hard constraint is provably built.

Final standing: 12 rules fully proven, week-boundary carry-out binding with its
release indicated but unproven, break concurrency present with warn semantics
proven, blank-interval staffing needing a purpose-built fixture, and the
coverage floor/target terms unprobeable because they are soft objective terms.

Zero new engine defects across all three rounds.

Seven of my probe constructions were invalid and every one failed the same way:
mutating a parsed contract so the model goes infeasible for a reason other than
the rule under test. That is the strongest argument yet for fix-plan W3,
purpose-built fixtures.

===== 3da490264a50fa31891db0012188e122e979716f
Author: Claude

W1 (staged): floor losses get a cap and a gate of their own

Register #1, the only finding with a live observation. Sweep case 2 lost 3 floor
intervals to break placement, the quality gate returned PASS, and the run's
floor came out one below the authors' baseline.

Three defects fixed together. floor_losses_from_breaks was computed, exported to
the leaderboard and used in a near-feasible test, but no release gate read it.
The one bound that touched it borrowed quality_max_target_losses_from_breaks --
no floor-loss cap existed anywhere in the contract. And that bound applied only
when target_loss_gate_mode == "fail", whose default is "warn", while the target
bound beside it is unconditional, so by default floor losses were unconstrained
in joint refinement while target losses were hard-capped.

The new field mirrors the target one exactly: same scaled default of
max(3, ceil(active * 0.05)), its own gate mode defaulting to warn, workbook
aliases, and a line in the contract payload. Verified parsing on all 15
workbooks. New code reads attributes directly rather than through the permissive
getattr defaults, per W4.

Two consequences the authors need to weigh, both deliberate.

The contract hash CHANGES. input_contract_payload gained two fields and the
payload is hashed into contract_sha256, hence into run_id. Every previous fix in
this work preserved the hash on all 14 workbooks; this one cannot, because a
setting that decides gate outcomes genuinely belongs in the contract identity.
The cost is that --resume against an existing work dir will refuse and anything
keyed on contract hash needs rebaselining. Excluding the fields from the hash
would be the wrong fix.

The joint-refinement bound is now unconditional, matching the target bound. That
is the one part that can change a produced schedule, so it needs its own A/B
with repeats -- multi-worker runs are nondeterministic and the effect is around
one interval.

Default chosen deliberately not to break anything: on AE_AR_Choice the floor cap
computes to 9, so its observed 3 losses are now REPORTED but do not trip the
gate. Making the loss visible is the fix; choosing a tighter floor budget is a
scheduling-policy call for the authors on more evidence than one observation.

One test needed updating and it is worth being explicit about why.
test_every_gate_routes_through_the_same_helper pins the exact roster of gates so
a new one cannot appear without a deliberate decision. floor_loss is such a
decision and it does route through apply(), so the guard's intent is preserved
exactly -- the list gains one entry and the assertion still proves every gate
uses the helper. The applier makes that edit itself so it cannot be forgotten.

Gate on the patched tree: 17 suites PASS, 2 selfchecks PASS, undefined-name
sweep PASS.

Staged, not applied. The sweep is on case 5 of 6.

W2 (staged): C-3, the preference vocabulary, completing the B-11 pair

Register #3. preference_kind recognised exactly two words, "off" and "leave".
Everything else returned "other", which falls through all three blocking
branches of associate_day_eligible_shifts, so the person is schedulable.
Annual Leave, A/L, AL, Vacation, Sick, Leave Day, OFF (approved), Off Day, Rest,
RD and X all produced no constraint and no warning, on a sheet that carries zero
data validations and whose own banner invites free text.

This is B-11 one level down -- B-11 catches an unmatched NAME, this catches a
matched name whose VALUE is not understood -- so fixing B-11 alone would have
left the cheaper half of the hole open.

Two parts, and the second matters more. The vocabulary now covers the forms
planners actually write, via LEAVE_WORDS and OFF_WORDS. And an unrecognised
non-blank preference or fixed cell is now a contract failure,
UNRECOGNISED_PREFERENCE_VALUE, naming the associate, the day, the sheet and the
value. Widening alone would only move the cliff; failing closed removes it.

Step two is implemented in validate_input_contract rather than in the sheet
parsers, deliberately: that keeps it independent of the B-11 applier, which
edits _parse_preferences and _parse_fixed_nesting. Verified that the two
appliers compose cleanly in either order.

Measured: 13 realistic synonyms now block; genuine typos like "Leve" become a
named contract failure instead of silence; and 0 of 15 shipped workbooks
regress, because every shipped workbook writes exactly "Leave" or "OFF" -- which
is precisely why C-3 stayed latent. It will fire the first time a human types
into the sheet the engine asks them to type into.

full gate after each one, stopping at the first failure so the tree is left at
the last good state and the failure is readable instead of buried under the next
change. --dry-run does the whole sequence against a copy.

Order matters and is encoded: W1, then B-12 before B-11 (B-11 false-positives on
two workbooks without the header fix), then C-3, then B-9, then copying the
staged test suites into the gate. W3's fixtures are inputs and need no
application.

It deliberately stops there. W4, W5, W6, W7b, W8, W9 and W11 each need a
decision or a measurement rather than a patch, and are listed at the end of the
run so they are not silently forgotten.

extra flags including a 900s joint-refinement reserve and a 300s coordinated
repair reserve. Those come off the top and Stage 1 is the remainder.

exactly this.

===== c8d28778686d221650b27752c5599b15170451a5
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-17T13:28:42+00:00

RC-B: align the shadow defaults instead of deleting them

The first version of this applier collapsed getattr(parsed, "x", shadow) to
parsed.x, reasoning that a declared dataclass field always exists so the shadow
is dead. The gate disproved that at once: callers pass duck-typed
SimpleNamespace stand-ins, not only real ParsedInput instances, so the shadow
genuinely fires. Eleven errors in test_rc9_2_1_selector_integrity.

That reframes the defect. It is not that a default exists, it is that its value
disagrees with the policy the dataclass declares, and in two cases with itself.
A stand-in missing the field silently receives the permissive answer: 999999
against a declared 3, 'off' against a declared 'fail', 2.0 against 1.35.

So the applier now rewrites the shadow to the declared default and keeps the
defensive read. 49 divergent sites aligned across 81 inspected.

Two further corrections while getting there, both found by reading the output
rather than by the gate. Writing a dataclasses field(default_factory=dict)
sentinel as a literal shadow would hand callers a Field object instead of a
dict, so those are skipped; and {} is equivalent to that declaration rather
than divergent from it, which an over-strict assertion had wrong. That
assertion firing also meant an earlier GATE PASS had run against an unpatched
tree and proved nothing.

Gate passes on the genuinely patched tree: 18 suites.

 evidence/POST_APPLY_AB_RESULT.md | 79 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 79 insertions(+)
===== 87379e9a78b53dfc73dc9e83208a0984bcbba054
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-17T18:40:18+00:00

#48 verdict: revert the unconditional joint-refinement floor bound

Nine paired runs across three seeds retract the headline result and reverse the
recommendation.

AE_IT_Choice's apparent unblocking was a lucky seed. At 9001 the unpatched arm
unblocks the same case on its own, and at 9002 neither arm does, so
blocked-versus-unblocked tracks the seed rather than the code.

Worse, the bound damages the floor it was meant to protect. AE_IT_B2B's
before_floor is pinned at exactly 91 on all three seeds in the patched arm
against 91, 98 and 97 unpatched, with a worse after-floor every time. The
mechanism is that before_floor_hit is a model.NewBoolVar rather than a measured
constant, so both sides of "after >= before - cap" are solver-controlled and the
constraint is satisfiable by lowering before. The solver took that route.

Reverts only the model constraint. W1's metric, cap, gate and canonical-surface
entry stay: they are reporting rather than search behaviour, and they found real
losses nobody was measuring. Gate passes at 18 suites.

The proper fix -- bounding against the incumbent's measured before-floor so a
degraded skeleton cannot buy slack -- is logged, not written, because it is a
new behavioural change needing its own A/B.

===== a279f7ec140f8bddb6291c3a0ae31c44a7462dab
Author: Claude

Close S6-1 and S6-2; reclassify S9-1 as behavioural

S6-1 and S6-2 were one defect seen from two sides. The model forces every
nesting-group member onto the leader's off, leave and shift variables, and
leave is pinned from the contract, so two members with different approved leave
give 1 == 0 and the model is INFEASIBLE with nothing naming the cause. The
existing check compared fixed_schedule only, and only for associates that had
one, while the model groups on nesting_group alone.

The fix mirrors the model's own constraint under the same fixed_enabled guard
and emits CONFLICTING_LEAVE_OR_OFF_IN_NESTING_GROUP. On the fixture built for
this case the contract goes from no failures to the named one; on the
15-workbook corpus nothing newly fails and no verdict changes.

S9-1 is reclassified rather than fixed. It was logged as after-break terms
needing a prefix, but measurement shows the eight terms have no before_ variant
anywhere in the engine, so there is nothing to switch to. The before-break
ranking really is scored partly on after-break quantities, and correcting that
means computing eight new metrics and changing candidate ranking -- a
behavioural change needing its own A/B, not a cleanup.

 evidence/NEXT_MOVES.md               |  54 +++++++++++++++++
 tools/apply_s132_delete_dead_code.py | 113 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 2 files changed, 167 insertions(+)
===== 3bc177e40eeea561b32acaaf2bcb596a55ca812c
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-17T23:16:13+00:00

B-13: the joint refinement bounds can be satisfied by degrading the schedule

Found while building the corrected version of the bound reverted earlier today,
and it is the more serious of the two because it is pre-existing and live.

The target bound is unconditional original code with the identical flaw the
floor bound was reverted for: both sides are model.NewBoolVar, so
"after >= before - cap" can be satisfied either by protecting the after-state
or by lowering the before-state, and the solver may take the cheaper route.
W1 did not invent that pattern; it copied the target bound beside it.

The three-seed measurement already showed the mechanism operating on the floor
bound, with AE_IT_B2B's before_floor pinned at 91 whenever the bound was active
against 91/98/97 when it was not.

The engine already contains the correct pattern twelve lines below: bounds
against min_after_target and min_after_floor, which are caller-supplied
integers and therefore cannot be gamed. Those are passed at two call sites and
left None at two others, and where they are None the only active protection is
the gameable one.

The fix is specified in the evidence file and deliberately not applied: a
behavioural change to the search, written at the end of a long session and
measured on one seed, is precisely how the reverted regression got in. The
mechanism is proven; the magnitude on production schedules is not, and that is
the next run rather than the next patch.

 evidence/B13_JOINT_BOUNDS_DEGRADE_BEFORE.md | 88 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 88 insertions(+)
===== 7fa16077e699cd9d088bde274e8938d668ecdc0c
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-18T00:02:11+00:00

Retract B-10: the break-slice constant is correctly a floor

B-10 claimed the 180-second constant was evidence for a cap being used as a
floor. That was a misreading of the engine's own probe.

The curve records 90 to 150 seconds returning 160 and 180 seconds returning
162. The plateau is the best value and 180 is where it is first reached, so a
shorter attempt does not fail, it ships a schedule two intervals worse. The
constant is correctly a floor as well as a cap.

The gate caught it at once, via tests that pin this deliberately with the probe
data in their docstring. Those tests exist because the same constant was wrong
once before, as the old 20-second floor fixed under A37, and they were written
to stop it regressing. They worked.

No code change. The applier is discarded rather than left where it could be
mistaken for pending work.

 evidence/B10_RETRACTED.md                | 43 +++++++++++++++++
 tools/apply_b13_constant_joint_bounds.py | 83 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 2 files changed, 126 insertions(+)
===== 404f3a3e3eb762c02c80bf850c34b0b097ae72a1
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-18T01:21:41+00:00

Correct S9-2: dominance decides a shipped artifact, not just alternatives

The register recorded S9-2 as affecting alternatives rather than the release
pick, and I repeated that earlier today while proposing to close it by tracing
instead of measuring. The trace disproved it.

The release selection computes a Pareto frontier, filters it to genuinely safer
options, and takes the recommendation from that set, falling back to the
lexicographic anchor only when the set is empty. The result is returned as
RECOMMENDED_FINAL and written as _RECOMMENDED_FINAL_AFTER_BREAKS_SCHEDULE.xlsx,
which is a release artifact.

Since dominance tests about 15 dimensions while the lexicographic order has
about 31 terms, a candidate can be non-dominated on what dominance sees and yet
rank below the anchor on what it does not. If it also passes the safety filter
it ships.

S9-2 therefore moves from trace-and-close to needing an A/B with repeats,
alongside B-13. Mechanism established by reading; magnitude unmeasured.

This is the third register description to prove wrong today, after S9-1 and
B-10. Remaining items should be re-derived from the source before work is
scheduled against them.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Claude-Session: https://claude.ai/code/session_01MH973LepRRvS7jQvRfGiqr


 evidence/S92_REACHES_THE_RELEASE_PICK.md | 58 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 58 insertions(+)
===== 00595a4a2c086269c94a0b536f25e94ace155d5e
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-18T01:36:09+00:00

Retract the S9-2 fix: the frontier overriding the ordering is the design

The earlier correction was right that dominance decides a shipped artifact. The
conclusion I drew from it, that this is a defect, was wrong.

The fix required frontier candidates to be no worse than the anchor on the full
quality tuple. The engine's selfcheck rejected it immediately: two selector
regressions that exist to pin the selector's ability to trade a little target
for more floor both fell back to the strict anchor.

The guard was incoherent. recommendation_anchor is the maximum of the
target-priority ordering by construction, so "not worse than the anchor on that
ordering" is satisfiable only by ties. It did not narrow the mechanism, it
disabled it.

Dominance disagreeing with the lexicographic order is the point: the ordering is
target-priority, the frontier surfaces candidates better on safety dimensions
target-priority under-weights, and genuinely_safer_than_strict already guards
the trade on four floor-safety dimensions.

Closed as not-a-defect; applier discarded. Whether those four dimensions are the
right guard is a scheduling-policy question for the authors, not an engineering
defect.

 evidence/S92_RETRACTED.md | 59 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++

===== 391793abdbadb882d9d495f315c9eeda36105e15
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-18T01:37:27+00:00

Retract S15-3: not truncating under-funded phases is documented design

S15-3 proposed skipping phases allocated below their own viability guard and
redistributing the seconds. The source states why that is deliberately not done:
nominal allocation is not the budget a phase receives, because earlier phases
return unspent time, measured at seventeen occurrences on NMG SP. A phase
nominally below its guard may be fully funded by carryover, and truncating on
the nominal figure would drop phases that would have run.

The recorded evidence never showed a live problem either. It is
target_lock_recovery at 43s against a 65s guard at a 600s total budget, while
task #37 had already measured the same phase at 139s against the same guard at
3600s. The condition appears only where every phase is starved, and the engine
prints BUDGET_PHASE_AT_RISK naming the phase and its shortfall.

That closes all four behavioural findings without a behavioural fix: S9-1
reclassified, B-10, S9-2 and S15-3 retracted. The only real defect in this area,
B-13, was not on the list and was found while building a fix for something else.

In each case the engine carried a comment or a test explaining the decision, and
the register had preserved the observation while losing the rationale.

===== 0dd3f9a6422261419ab7bbd3b47d918e612e5d5d
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-18T03:17:45+00:00

B-13 structural probe: the gameable bound is equivalent to no bound

The end-to-end A/B could not complete. Container restarts moved from roughly
hourly to every 10-30 minutes and killed four runs simultaneously 5.5 minutes
into a wave. Not memory, with 15 GB free; the kernel log shows idle-reclaim
recycling the container.

B-13's claim is structural rather than statistical, so it was put to CP-SAT
directly. Rebuilding the engine's exact shape and comparing three cases:

    no bound at all              before_hits 0, after_hits 0
    gameable after >= before-cap before_hits 0, after_hits 0
    constant after >= 10-cap     before_hits 8, after_hits 8

The gameable form gives exactly the answer that having no constraint gives. It
is not weak protection, it is none: whenever anything would lower the
before-state, the bound moves with it and never binds.

The probe does not show the engine's full objective always chooses to degrade,
and the evidence file says so. That part rests on the three paired seeds already
run, where AE_IT_B2B's before_floor was pinned at 91 with the bound active and
ranged 91/98/97 without it.

The fix stays unapplied: it changes search behaviour, and while the evidence for
the defect is now strong, the evidence that the fix is neutral-or-better on real
schedules needs an end-to-end A/B in an environment where runs survive.

===== 66ee81b6c15821daf3f416fa0240959a83bacdcf
Author: Claude
Date:   2026-09-18T04:23:49+00:00

Correct the overnight diagnosis: idle reaping, not container restarts

I attributed four failed A/B attempts to container restarts. The timings
disprove it: waves launched at 02:37 and 03:50 both stopped writing exactly 5.5
minutes later, while the restarts were at 03:12 and 04:22, long after the runs
were already dead. I checked uptime, saw a recent restart, and stopped looking
-- twice.

The real pattern is that background work is reaped a few minutes after the
session goes idle. The earlier sweeps in this work ran for hours because the
session was continuously active across many turns; once check-ins moved to
30-minute intervals, each launch got about five minutes and was collected.

Long background work is therefore not possible here while the session idles, and
scheduled check-ins only wake up to find it gone. A fifth relaunch would fail
identically, so it was stopped rather than repeated.

None of the B-13 conclusions depend on the overnight A/B, which produced
nothing. They rest on the structural probes and on the three paired seeds
measured while the session was active.

Chunked resume also cannot reach joint refinement, and the reason is structural

The engine supports --resume against a registry, so a 3600-second plan was
